Residuum Israel non perpetrabunt iniquitatem (<sty c="000000" i="1">hoc est</sty>, reliquae; <sty c="000000" i="1">ad</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">verbum est</sty>, residuum; <sty c="000000" i="1">sed quia nomen</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">est collectivum</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">ideo mutatur numerus</sty>,) et non loquentur mendacium, et non invenietur in ore ipsorum lingua dolosa (<sty c="000000" i="1">vel</sty>, lingua fraudis;) quoniam ipsi pascentur et accubabunt; et nemo erit exterrens.

[BODY] Here the Prophet pursues the same subject — that God would provide for the safety of his Church, by cutting off the majority of the people, and by reserving a few; for his purpose was to gather for himself a pure and holy Church, as the city had previously been full of all uncleanness. It ought, then, to have been a compensation to ease their grief, when the godly saw that God would be propitious to them, though he had treated them with great severity. And we must bear in mind what I have before stated — that the Church could not have been preserved without correcting and subduing that arrogance, which arose from a false profession as to God. Zephaniah takes it now as granted, that pride could not be torn away from their hearts, except they were wholly cast down, and thus made contrite. He then teaches us, that as long as they remained whole, they were ever proud, and that hence it was necessary to apply a violent remedy, that they might learn meekness and humility; which he intimates when he says, <sty c="800000" i="1">that the residue of the people would be humble and afflicted</sty>; for if they had become willingly teachable, there would have been no need of so severe a correction. In short, though the faithful lament that God should thus almost annihilate his Church, yet in order that they might not murmur, he shows that this was a necessary remedy. How so? because they would have always conducted themselves arrogantly against God, had they not

--- PAGE 100 ---

[BODY] been afflicted. It was, therefore, needful for them to be in a manner broken, because they could not be bent. <sty c="800000" i="1">I will</sty>, then, he says, <sty c="800000" i="1">make the residue an</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">afflicted and a poor people</sty>.
[BODY] The word, <sty c="008080" i="0">yn[</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">oni</sty>, means humble; but as he adds the word <sty c="008080" i="0">ld</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">dal</sty>, he no doubt shows that the Jews could not be corrected without being stripped of all the materials of their glorying.<sty c="800000" i="0"> f50</sty> They were, indeed, extremely wedded to their boastings; yea, they were become hardened in their contempt of God. He therefore says, that this fruit would at last follow, that they would <sty c="800000" i="1">trust in the Lord</sty>, that is, when he had laid them prostrate.

[BODY] This verse contains a most useful instruction: for first we are taught that the Church is subdued by the cross, that she may know her pride, which is so innate and so fixed in the hearts of men, that it cannot be removed, except the Lord, so to speak, roots it out by force. There is then no wonder that the faithful are so much humbled be the Lord, and that the lot of the Church is so contemptible; for if they had more vigor, they would soon, as is often the case, break out into an insolent spirit. That the Lord, then, may keep his elect under restraint, he subdues and tames them by poverty. In short, he exercises them under the cross. This is one thing.

[BODY] We must also notice the latter clause, when he says, <sty c="800000" i="1">They shall trust in the</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">Lord</sty>, that is, those who have been reduced to poverty and want. We hence see for what purpose God deprives us of all earthly trust, and takes away from us every ground of glorying; it is, that we may rely only on his favor. This dependence ought not, indeed, to be extorted from us, for what can be more desirable than to trust in God? But while men arrogate to themselves more than what is right, and thus put themselves in the place of God, they cannot really and sincerely trust in him. They indeed imagine that they trust in God, when they ascribe to him a part of their salvation; but except this be done wholly, no trust can be placed in God. It is hence necessary that they who ascribe to themselves even the smallest thing, should be reduced to nothing: and this is what the Prophet means. Let us further know, that men do not profit under God’s scourges, except they wholly deny themselves, and forget their own power, which they falsely imagine, and recomb on him alone.

[BODY] But the Prophet speaks of the elect alone; for we see that many are severely afflicted, and are not softened, nor do they put off their former hardihood. But the Lord so chastises his people, that by the spirit of

--- PAGE 101 ---

[BODY] meekness he corrects in them all pride and haughtiness. But by saying, <sty c="800000" i="1">They shall trust in the name of Jehovah</sty>, he sets this trust in contrast with the pride which he had previously condemned. They indeed wished to appear to trust in the name of God, when they boasted of Mount Sion, and haughtily brought forward the adoption by which they had been separated from heathen nations; but it was a false boasting, which had no trust in it. To trust, then, in the name of Jehovah is nothing else than sincerely to embrace the favor which he offers in his word, and not to make vain pretenses, but to call on him with a pure heart and with a deep feeling of penitence.

[BODY] For the same purpose he adds, <sty c="800000" i="1">The residue of Israel shall no more work</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">iniquity nor speak falsehood; nor shall there be found a deceitful tongue in</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">their mouth</sty>. The Prophet continues the same subject — that the Church is not to be less esteemed when it consists only of a few men; for in the vast number there was great filth, which not only polluted the earth by its ill savor, but infected heaven itself. Since then Jerusalem was full of iniquities, as long as the people remained entire, the Prophet adduces this comfort, that there was no reason for sorrow, if from a vast number as the sand of the sea, and from a great multitude like the stars, God would only collect a small band; for by this means the Church would be cleansed. And it was of great importance that the filth should be cleansed from God’s sanctuary; for what could have been more disgraceful than that the holy place should be made the lodging of swine, and that the place which God designed to be consecrated to himself, should be profaned? As then Jerusalem was the sanctuary of God, ought not true religion to have flourished there? But when it became polluted with every kind of filth, the Prophet shows that it ought not to have seemed grievous that the Lord should take away that vast multitude which falsely boasted that they professed his name. <sty c="800000" i="1">They</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">shall not </sty>then <sty c="800000" i="1">work iniquity</sty>.

[BODY] Under one kind of expression he includes the whole of a righteous life, when he says, <sty c="800000" i="1">They shall not speak falsely, nor will there be found a</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">deceitful tongue</sty>. It is indeed sufficient for the practice of piety or integrity of life to keep the tongue free from frauds and falsehood; but as it cannot be that any one will abstain from all frauds and falsehood, except he purely and from the heart fears God, the Prophet, by including the whole under one thing, expresses under the word tongue what embraces complete holiness of life.

--- PAGE 102 ---

[BODY] It may be now asked, whether this has ever been fulfilled. It is indeed certain, that though few returned to their own country, there were yet many hypocrites among that small number; for as soon as the people reached their own land, every one, as we find, was so bent on his own advantages, that they polluted themselves with heathen connections, that they neglected the building of the temple, and deprived the priests of their tenths, that they became cold in the worship of God. With these things they were charged by Haggai, Zechariah, and Malachi. Since these things were so, what means this promise, that there would be no iniquity when God had cleansed his Church? The Prophet speaks comparatively; for the Lord would so cleanse away the spots from his people that their holiness would then appear more pure. Though then many hypocrites were still mixed with the good and real children of God, it was yet true that iniquity was not so prevalent, that frauds and falsehood were not so rampant among the people as they were before.

[BODY] He afterwards adds, <sty c="800000" i="1">For they shall feed and lie down, and there will be</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">none to terrify them</sty>. He mentions another benefit from God — that he will protect his people from all wrongs when they had repented. We must ever bear in mind what I have stated — that the Prophet intended here to heal the sorrow of the godly, which night have otherwise wholly dejected their minds. That he might then in some measure alleviate the grief of God’s children, he brings forward this argument — “Though few shall remain, it is yet well that the Lord will cleanse away the filth of the holy city, that it may be justly deemed to be God’s habitation, which was before the den of thieves. It is not then a loss to you, that few will dwell in the holy land, for God will be a faithful guardian of your safety. What need then is there of a large multitude, except to render you safe from enemies and from wild beasts? What does it signify, if God receives you under his protection, under the condition that ye shall be secure, though not able to resist your enemies? Though one cannot defend another, yet if God be your protector, and ye be made to live in peace under the defense which he promises, there is no reason why ye should say, that you have suffered a great loss, when your great number was made small. It is then enough for you to live under God’s guardianship; for though the whole world were united against you, and ye had no strength nor defense yourselves, yet the Lord can preserve you; <sty c="800000" i="1">there will be no one to terrify you</sty>.

--- PAGE 103 ---

[BODY] And this argument is taken from the law; for it is mentioned among other blessings, that God would render safe the life of his people; which is an invaluable blessing, and without which the life of men, we know, must be miserable; for nothing is more distressing than constant fear, and nothing is more conducive to happiness than a quiet life: and hence to live in quietness and free from all fear, is what the Lord promises as a chief blessing to his people.

[CENTERED] PRAYER

[CENTERED] <sty c="000080" i="0">Grant, Almighty God, that since the depravity of our nature is so</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">great, that we cannot bear prosperity without some wantonness of</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">the flesh immediately raging in us, and without becoming even</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">arrogant against thee, — O grant, that we may profit under the</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">trials of the cross; and when thou have blest us, may we with</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">lowly hearts, renouncing our perverseness, submit ourselves to</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">thee, and not only bear thy yoke submissively, but proceed in this</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">obedience all our life, and so contend against all temptations as</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">never to glory in ourselves, and feel also convinced, that all true and</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">real glory is laid up for us in thee, until we shall enjoy it in thy</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">celestial kingdom, through Christ our Lord. Amen.</sty>

[CENTERED_H2] <sty c="006411" i="0">LECTURE ONE HUNDRED AND TWENTY-SEVENTH</sty>

[H2] <sty c="800000" i="0"><360314></sty>ZEPHANIAH 3:14, 15
[BODY] 14. Sing, O daughter of Zion; shout, O Israel; be glad and rejoice with all the heart, O daughter of Jerusalem.
[RIGHT] 14. Exulta filia Sion (<sty c="000000" i="1">vel</sty>, jubila; exulta Israel; gaude et exulta toto corde filia Ierjusalem.

[INDENT] 15. The LORD hath taken away thy judgments, he hath cast out thine enemy: the king of Israel, <sty c="000000" i="1">even</sty> the LORD, <sty c="000000" i="1">is</sty> in the midst of thee: thou shalt not see evil any more.
[RIGHT] 15. Abstulit Iehova judicia tua, purgando avertit inimicos tuos; rex Israel Iehova in medio tui; non videbis malum amplius.

[BODY] THE Prophet confirms what he has been teaching, and encourages the faithful to rejoice, as though he saw with his eyes what he had previously promised. For thus the Prophets, while encouraging the faithful to entertain hope, stimulate them to testify their gratitude, as though God’s favor was already enjoyed. It is certain, that this instruction was set before the Jews for this purpose, — that in their exile and extreme distress they might yet prepare themselves to give thanks to God, as though they were already, as they say, in possession of what they had prayed for. But we must remember the design of our Prophet, and the common mode of proceeding which all the Prophets followed; for the faithful are exhorted to praise God the same as if they had already enjoyed his blessings, which yet were remote, and seemed concealed from their view.

[BODY] We now then perceive what the Prophet meant in encouraging the Jews to praise God: he indeed congratulates them as though they were already enjoying that happiness, which was yet far distant: but as it is a congratulation only, we must also bear in mind, that God deals so bountifully with his Church as to stimulate the faithful to gratitude; for we pollute all his benefits, except we return for them, as it has been stated elsewhere, the sacrifice of praise: and as a confirmation of this is the repetition found here, which would have otherwise appeared superfluous.

--- PAGE 105 ---

[BODY] “Exult, daughter of Sion, shout, be glad; rejoice with all thine heart, daughter of Jerusalem.”<sty c="800000" i="0"> f51</sty>

[BODY] But the Prophet was not thus earnest without reason; for he saw how difficult it was to console the afflicted, especially when God manifested no evidence of hope according to the perception of the flesh; but his purpose was by this heap of words to fortify them, that they might with more alacrity struggle with so many hard and severe trials.

[BODY] He then adds, that God had <sty c="800000" i="1">taken away the judgments </sty>of Zion. By judgments, he means those punishments which would have been inflicted if it had been the Lord’s purpose to deal according to strict justice with the Jews, as when any one says in our language, <sty c="000000" i="1">J’ai brule tous tes proces</sty>. He intimates then that God would no more make an enquiry as to the sins of his people. The word <sty c="008080" i="0">fpçm</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">meshiphath</sty>, we know, has various meanings in Hebrew; but in this place, as I have said, it means what we call in French, <sty c="000000" i="1">Toutes procedures</sty>. In short, God declares that the sins of his people are buried, so that he in a manner cuts off his character as a judge, and remits his own right, so that he will no more contend with the Jews, or summon them, as they say, to trial. <sty c="800000" i="1">Jehovah</sty> then <sty c="800000" i="1">will take away thy</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">judgments</sty>.<sty c="800000" i="0"> f52</sty>

[BODY] Then follows an explanation, <sty c="800000" i="1">By clearing he has turned aside all enemies</sty>;
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">f53</sty> for we know that war is one of God’s judgments. As then God had
[BODY] punished the Jews by the Assyrians, by the Egyptians, by the Chaldeans, and by other heathen nations, he says now, that all enemies would be turned away. It hence follows, that neither the Assyrians nor the Chaldeans had assailed them merely through their own inclination, but that they were, according to what has been elsewhere stated, the swords, as it were, of God.

[BODY] It afterwards follows, <sty c="800000" i="1">The king of Israel is Jehovah in the midst of thee</sty>. Here the Prophet briefly shows, that the sum of real and true happiness is then possessed, when God declares, that he undertakes the care of his people. God is said to be in the midst of us, when he testifies that we live under his guardianship and protection. Properly speaking, he never forsakes his own; but these forms of speech, we know, are to be referred to the perception of the flesh. When the Lord is said to be afar off, or to dwell in the midst of us, it is to be understood with reference to our ideas: for we think God to be then absent when he gives liberty to our enemies, and we

--- PAGE 106 ---

[BODY] seem to be exposed as a prey to them; but God is said to dwell in the midst of us when he protects us by his power, and turns aside all assaults. Thus, then, our Prophet now says, that God will be in the midst of his Church; for he would really and effectually prove that he is the guardian of his elect people. He had been indeed for a time absent, when his people were deprived of all help, according to what Moses expresses when he says, that the people had denuded themselves, because they had renounced God, by whose hand they had been safely protected, and were also to be protected to the end. <sty c="800000" i="0"><023225></sty>Exodus 32:25.

[BODY] He lastly adds, <sty c="800000" i="1">Thou shalt not see evil</sty>. Some read, “Thou shalt not fear evil,” by inserting <sty c="008080" i="0">y</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">iod</sty>; but the meaning is the same: for the verb, to see, in Hebrew is, we know, often to be taken in the sense of finding or experiencing. <sty c="800000" i="1">Thou shalt </sty>then <sty c="800000" i="1">see no evil</sty>; that is, God will cause thee to live in quietness, free from every disturbance. If the other reading, Thou shalt not fear evil, be preferred, then the reference is to the blessing promised in the law; for nothing is more desirable than peace and tranquillity. Since then this is the chief of temporal blessings, the Prophet does not without reason say, that the Church would be exempt from all fear and anxiety, when God should dwell in the midst of it, according to what he says in <sty c="800000" i="0"><194601></sty>Psalm 46:1. It now follows —

[H2] <sty c="800000" i="0"><360316></sty>ZEPHANIAH 3:16, 17
[BODY] 16. In that day it shall be said to Jerusalem, Fear thou not: <sty c="000000" i="1">and to</sty> Zion, Let not thine hands be slack.
[INDENT] 16. In die illa dicitur Jerosolymae, Ne timeas; Sion, ne pigrescant (<sty c="000000" i="1">vel</sty>, solvantur, <sty c="000000" i="1">name </sty>[<sty c="008080" i="0">hpr</sty>] <sty c="000000" i="1">significat</sty> lentum esse, <sty c="000000" i="1">vel</sty>, remissum, <sty c="000000" i="1">vel</sty>, dissolutum; ne <sty c="000000" i="1">ergo</sty> pigrescant) manus tuae. 17. The LORD thy God in the midst of thee <sty c="000000" i="1">is</sty> mighty; he will save, he will rejoice over thee with joy; he will rest in his love, he will joy over thee with singing.
[RIGHT] 17. Iehova Deus tuus in medio tui fortis servabit; exultabit (<sty c="000000" i="1">vel</sty>, gaudebit) super te in laetitia; quiescet (silebit <sty c="000000" i="1">ad verbum</sty>,<sty c="000000" i="1"> vel</sty>, quietus erit) in amore suo; exultabit super te cum jubilatione.

--- PAGE 107 ---

[BODY] The Prophet proceeds still to confirm the same truth, but employs a different mode of speaking. It shall, he says, be then said everywhere to Zion, <sty c="800000" i="1">Fear not, let not thine hands be let down</sty>, etc. For these words may no less suitably be applied to the common report or applause of all men, then to the prophetic declaration; so that the expression, It shall be said, may be the common congratulation, which all would vie to offer. The import of the whole is, that Jerusalem would be so tranquil that either the Prophets, or all with common consent would say, “Thou enjoyest thy rest: for God really shows that he cares for thee; there is therefore no cause for thee hereafter to fear.” For there is expressed here a real change: since the Jews had been before in daily fear, the Prophet intimates, that they would be so safe from every danger, as to be partakers of the long-wishedfor rest, with the approbation even of the whole world. Hence, it shall be said — by whom? either by the Prophets, or by common report: it makes no great difference, whether there would be teachers to announce their state joyful and prosperous, or whether all men would, by common consent, applaud God’s favor, when he had removed from his people all wars, troubles, and fears, so as to make them live in quietness.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">It shall </sty>then <sty c="800000" i="1">be said to Jerusalem, fear not; Sion! let not thine hands be</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">relaxed</sty>. By saying Fear not, and let not thine hands be relaxed, he intimates, that all vigor is so relaxed by fear, that no member can perform its function. But by taking a part for the whole, he understands by the word hands, every other part of the body; for by the hands men perform their works. Hence in Scripture the hands often signify the works of men. The meaning then is — that God’s Church would then be in such a state of quietness as to be able to discharge all its duties and transact its concerns peaceably and orderly. And it is what we also know by experience, that when fear prevails in our hearts we are as it were lifeless, so that we cannot raise even a finger to do anything: but when hope animates us, there is a vigor in the whole body, so that alacrity appears everywhere. The Prophet, no doubt, means here, that God thus succors his elect, not that they may indulge in pleasures, as is too often the case, but that they may, on the contrary, strenuously devote themselves to the performance of their duties. We ought therefore to notice the connection between a tranquil state and diligent hands; for, as I have said, God does not free us from all trouble and fear, that we may grow torpid in our pleasures, but that we

--- PAGE 108 ---

[BODY] may, on the contrary, be more attentive to our duty. <sty c="800000" i="1">Sion</sty>, then! <sty c="800000" i="1">let thine</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">hands be no more torpid — </sty>Why?

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">Jehovah</sty>, he says, <sty c="800000" i="1">in the midst of thee strong, will save</sty>. He repeats what he had said, but more fully expresses what might have appeared obscure on account of its brevity. He therefore shows here more at large the benefit of God’s presence — that God will not dwell idly in his Church, but will be accompanied with his power. For what end? To save. We hence see that the word <sty c="008080" i="0">rwbg</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">gebur</sty>, ascribed to God, is very emphatical; as though he had said, that God would not be idle while residing in the midst of his Church, but would become its evident strength. And it is worthy of notice, that God exhibits not himself as strong that he may terrify his elect, but only that he may become their preserver.

[BODY] He afterwards adds, <sty c="800000" i="1">He will rejoice over thee with gladness</sty>. This must be referred to the gratuitous love of God, by which he embraces and cherishes his Church, as a husband his wife whom he most tenderly loves. Such feelings, we know, belong not to God; but this mode of speaking, which often occurs in Scripture, is thus to be understood by us; for as God cannot otherwise show his favor towards us and the greatness of his love, he compares himself to a husband, and us to a wife. He means in short — that God is most highly pleased when he can show himself kind to his Church.

[BODY] He confirms and shows again the same thing more clearly, <sty c="800000" i="1">He will be at rest</sty> (or silent) <sty c="800000" i="1">in his love</sty>. The proper meaning of <sty c="008080" i="0">çrj</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">charesh</sty>, is to be silent, but it means here to be at rest. The import is, that God will be satisfied, as we say in French, <sty c="000000" i="1">Il prendra tout son contentement</sty>; as though he had said that God wished nothing more than sweetly and quietly to cherish his Church. As I have already said, this feeling is indeed ascribed to God with no strict correctness; for we know that he can instantly accomplish whatever it pleases him: but he assumes the character of men; for except he thus speaks familiarly with us, he cannot fully show how much he loves us. God then shall be at rest in his love; that is, “It will be his great delight, it will be the chief pleasure of thy God when he cherishes thee: as when one cherishes a wife most dear to him, so God will then rest in his love.” <sty c="800000" i="1">He then says, He will exult over thee with joy</sty>.<sty c="800000" i="0"> f54</sty>

[BODY] These hyperbolic terms seem indeed to set forth something inconsistent, for what can be more alien to God’s glory than to exult like man when

--- PAGE 109 ---

[BODY] influenced by joy arising from love? It seems then that the very nature of God repudiates these modes of speaking, and the Prophet appears as though he had removed God from his celestial throne to the earth. A heathen poet says, —

[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Not well do agree, nor dwell on the same throne,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Majesty and love. (Ovid. Met. Lib. 2: 816-7.)</sty>

[BODY] God indeed represents himself here as a husband, who burns with the greatest love towards his wife; and this does not seem, as we have said, to be suitable to his glory; but whatever tends to this end — to convince us of God’s ineffable love towards us, so that we may rest in it, and being weaned as it were from the world, may seek this one thing only, that he may confer on us his favor — whatever tends to this, doubtless illustrates the glory of God, and derogates nothing from his nature. We at the same time see that God, as it were, humbles himself; for if it be asked whether these things are suitable to the nature of God, we must say, that nothing is more alien to it. It may then appear by no means congruous, that God should be described by us as a husband who burns with love to his wife: but we hence more fully learn, as I have already said, how great is God’s favor towards us, who thus humbles himself for our sake, and in a manner transforms himself, while he puts on the character of another. Let every one of us come home also to himself, and acknowledge how deep is the root of unbelief; for God cannot provide for our good and correct this evil, to which we are all subject, without departing as it were from himself, that he might come nigher to us.

[BODY] And whenever we meet with this mode of speaking, we ought especially to remember, that it is not without reason that God labors so much to persuade us of his love, because we are not only prone by nature to unbelief, but exposed to the deceits of Satan, and are also inconstant and easily drawn away from his word: hence it is that he assumes the character of man. We must, at the same time, observe what I have before stated — that whatever is calculated to set forth the love of God, does not derogate from his glory; for his chief glory is that vast and ineffable goodness by which he has once embraced us, and which he will show us to the end.

[BODY] What the Prophet says of <sty c="800000" i="1">that day </sty>is to be extended to the whole kingdom of Christ. He indeed speaks of the deliverance of the people; but we must ever bear in mind what I have already stated — that it is not one year, or a

--- PAGE 110 ---

[BODY] few years, which are intended, when the Prophets speak of future redemption; for the time which is now mentioned began when the people were restored from the Babylonian captivity, and continues its course to the final advent of Christ. And hence also we learn that these hyperbolic expressions are not extravagant, when the Prophets say, Thou shalt not afterwards fear, nor see evil: for if we regard the dispersion of that people, doubtless no trial, however heavy, can happen to us, which is not moderate, when we compare our lot with the state of the ancient people; for the land of Canaan was then the only pledge of God’s favor and love. When, therefore, the Jews were ejected from their inheritance, it was, as we have said elsewhere, a sort of repudiation; it was the same as if a father were to eject from his house a son, and to repudiate him. Christ was not as yet manifested to the world. The miserable Jews had an evidence, in figures and shadows, of that future favor which was afterwards manifested by the gospel. Since, then, God gave them so small an evidence of his love, how could it be otherwise but that they must have fainted, when driven far away from their land? Though the Church is now scattered and torn, and seems little short of being ruined, yet God is ever present with us in his only-begotten Son: we have also the gate of the celestial kingdom fully opened. There is, therefore, administered to us at all times more abundant reasons for joy than formerly to the ancient people, especially when they seemed to have been rejected by God. This is the reason why the Prophet says, that the Church would be lessened by calamities, when God again gathered it. But that redemption of the people of Israel ought at this day to be borne in mind by us; for it was a memorable work of God, by which he intended to afford a perpetual testimony that he is the deliverer of all those who hope in him. It follows —

[H2] <sty c="800000" i="0"><360318></sty>ZEPHANIAH 3:18

[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 18|||EN|||I will gather <sty c="000000" i="1">them that are</sty> sorrowful for the solemn assembly, <sty c="000000" i="1">who</sty> are of thee, <sty c="000000" i="1">to</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">whom</sty> the reproach of it <sty c="000000" i="1">was</sty> a burden.|||LA|||Afflictos a tempore (<sty c="000000" i="1">vel</sty>, pro tempore, <sty c="000000" i="1">vel</sty>, ad tempus, <sty c="000000" i="1">ut</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">alii vertunt</sty>) congregabo qui ex te erunt: onus (<sty c="000000" i="1">vertunt quidam</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">sed active</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">accipere Propheta</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">potius</sty>, qui sistinuerunt <sty c="000000" i="1">ergo</sty>) super eam opprobrium.

--- PAGE 111 ---

[BODY] He proceeds here with the same subject, but in different words; for except some consolation had been introduced, what the Prophet has hitherto said would have been frigid; for he had promised them joy, he had exhorted the chosen of God to offer praise and thanksgiving; but they were at the same time in a most miserable state. It was hence necessary to add this declaration respecting the exiles being gathered.

[BODY] But he says <sty c="800000" i="1">at the time</sty>. Some read, in respect to time; but this is obscure and strained. Others render it, at the time; but it means strictly from the time; though <sty c="008080" i="0">m</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">mem</sty>, may sometimes be rendered as a particle of comparison. Interpreters do not seem to me rightly to understand the Prophet’s meaning: for I do not doubt but that he points out here the fixed time of deliverance, as though he had said, I will again gather thine afflicted, and those who have endured thy reproach. When? at the time, <sty c="008080" i="0">d[wmm</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">memuod</sty>; that is, at the determined or fixed time: for <sty c="008080" i="0">d[wm</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">muod</sty>, is not taken in Hebrew for time simply, but for a predetermined time, as we say in French, <sty c="000000" i="1">Un terme prefix</sty>. <sty c="800000" i="1">I will </sty>then <sty c="800000" i="1">gather thine afflicted</sty>, but not soon. Our Prophet then holds the faithful here somewhat in suspense, that they might continue in their watch tower, and patiently wait for God’s help; for we know how great is our haste, and how we run headlong when we hope for anything; but this celerity, according to the old proverb, is often delay to us. Since, then, men are always carried away by a certain heat, or by too much impetuosity, to lay hold on what may happen, the Prophet here lays a restraint, and intimates that God has his own seasons to fulfill what he has promised, that he will not do so soon, nor according to the will of men, but when the suitable time shall come. And this time is that which he has appointed, not what we desire.

[BODY] He then adds, <sty c="800000" i="1">Who have sustained reproach for her</sty>. In this second clause the Prophet no doubt repeats the same thing; but at the same time he points out, not without reason, their condition — that the Jews suffered reproach and contumely at the time of their exile, and that on account of being the Church; that is, because they professed to worship their own God; for on account of his name the Jews were hated by all nations, inasmuch as their religion was different from the superstitions of all heathens. It could not hence be, but that the unbelieving should vex them with many reproaches, when they were carried away into exile, and scattered in all directions.<sty c="800000" i="0"> f55</sty>

--- PAGE 112 ---

[BODY] He had said before, I will gather the afflicted; but he now adds, I will gather those who have sustained reproach. I have stated that some read, A burden upon her is reproach; but no sense can be elicited from such words. The Prophet does here no doubt obviate a temptation which awaited God’s children, who would have to experience in exile what was most grievous to be borne; for they were to be exposed to the taunts and ridicule of all nations. Hence he seasonably heals their grief by saying, that though for a time they would be laughed at by the ungodly, they would yet return to their own country; for the Lord had resolved to gather them. But we must ever remember what I have said — that God would do this in his own time, when he thought it seasonable. It follows —

[H2] <sty c="800000" i="0"><360319></sty>ZEPHANIAH 3:19

[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 19|||EN|||Behold, at that time I will undo all that afflict thee: and I will save her that halteth, and gather her that was driven out; and I will get them praise and fame in every land where they have been put to shame.|||LA|||Ecce ego conficiens omnes oppressores tuos (qui te humiliant, ad verbum) in tempore illo; et servabo claudicantem, et reducam expulsam ad faciendum eos in laudem et nomen in terra opprobrii ipsorum.
[BODY] He confirms here what I have referred to in the last verse that God would overcome all obstacles, when his purpose was to restore his people. On this the Prophet, as we have said, dwells, that the Jews might in their exile sustain themselves with the hope of deliverance. As, then, they could not instantly conceive what was so incredible according to the perceptions of the flesh, he testifies that there is sufficient power in God to subdue all enemies.

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">At that time</sty>, he says, he repeats what had been stated before — that his people must wait as long as God pleases to exercise them under the cross; for if their option had been given to the Jews, they would have willingly continued at their ease; and we know how men are wont to exempt themselves from every trouble, fear, and sorrow. As therefore men naturally desire rest and immunity from all evil, the Prophet here exhorts the faithful to patience, and shows, that it cannot be that God will become

--- PAGE 113 ---

[BODY] their deliverer, except they submit to his chastisement; at that time then. It is ever to be observed, that the Prophet condemns that extreme haste which usually takes hold of men when God chastises them. However slowly then and gradually God proceeds in the work of delivering his own, the Prophet shows here, that there was no reason for them to despair, or to be broken down in their spirits.<sty c="800000" i="0"> f56</sty>

[BODY] He then subjoins, that he would <sty c="800000" i="1">save the halting, and restore the driven</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">away</sty>. By these words he means, that though the Church would be maimed and torn, there would yet be nothing that could hinder God to restore her: for by the halting and the driven away he understands none other than one so stripped of power as wholly to fail in himself. He therefore compares the Church of God to a person, who, with relaxed limbs, is nearly dead. Hence, when we are useless as to any work, what else is our life but a languor like to death? But the Prophet declares here, that the seasonable time would come when God would relieve his own people: though they were to become prostrate and fallen, though they were to be scattered here and there, like a torn body of man, an arm here and a leg there, every limb separated; yet he declares that nothing could possibly prevent God to gather his Church and restore it to its full vigor and strength. In short, he means that the restoration of the Church would be a kind of resurrection; for the Lord would humble his people until they became almost lifeless, so as not to be able to breathe: but he would at length gather them, and so gather them that they would not only breathe but be replenished with such new vigor as though they had received no loss. I cannot finish the whole today.

[CENTERED] PRAYER

[CENTERED] <sty c="000080" i="0">Grant, Almighty God, that as we are at this day so scattered on</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">account of our sins, and even they who seem to be collected in thy</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">name and under thy authority, are yet so torn by mutual discords,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">that the safety of thy Church hangs as it were on a thread, while in</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">the meantime thine enemies seem with savage cruelty to destroy all</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">those who are thine, and to obliterate thy gospel, — O grant, that</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">we may live in quietness and resignation, hoping in thy promises,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">so that we may not doubt, but that thou in due time will become</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">our deliverer: and may we so patiently bear to be afflicted and cast</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">down by thee, that we may ever raise up our groans to heaven so</sty>

--- PAGE 114 ---

[CENTERED] <sty c="000080" i="0">as to be heard through the name of thy Son, until being at length</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">freed from every contest, we shall enjoy that blessed rest which is</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">laid up for us in heaven, and which thine only begotten Son has</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">procured for us. Amen.</sty>

--- PAGE 115 ---

[CENTERED_H2] <sty c="006411" i="0">LECTURE ONE HUNDRED AND TWENTY-EIGHTH</sty>

[BODY] WE stopped yesterday at the latter clause of the last verse but one of the Prophet Zephaniah, where God promises that the Jews, who had been before not only obscure, but also exposed to all kinds of reproaches, would again become illustrious; for to give them for a name and for a praise, is no other thing than to render them celebrated, that they might be, as they say, in the mouth of every one.

[BODY] And he says, <sty c="800000" i="1">in the land of their shame</sty>, or reproach; for they had been a mockery everywhere; as the unbelieving thought that they deluded themselves with a vain hope, because they boasted that God, under whose protection they lived, would be their perpetual guardian, though they were driven away into exile. Hence an occasion for taunt and ridicule was given. But a change for the better is here promised; for all in Assyria and Chaldea would have to see that this was a people chosen by God; so that there would be a remarkable testimony among all nations, that all who trust in God are by no means disappointed, for they find that he is faithful in his promises. The last verse follows —

[H2] <sty c="800000" i="0"><360320></sty>ZEPHANIAH 3:20

[SCRIPTURE_ROW] 20|||EN|||At that time will I bring you <sty c="000000" i="1">again</sty>, even in the time that I gather you: for I will make you a name and a praise among all people of the earth, when I turn back your captivity before your eyes, saith the LORD.|||LA|||In tempore illo reducam vos, in tempore illo colligam vos; quia ponam vos in nomen et laudem per cunctos populos terrae, (<sty c="000000" i="1">vel</sty>, inter cunctos populos terrae,) quam reducam captivitates vestras in oculis vestris, dicit Jehova.
[BODY] He repeats the same things, with some change in the words; and not without reason, because no one of then thought that the Jews, who were cast as it were into the grave, would ever come forth again, and especially, that they would be raised unto such dignity and unto so elevated an honor., As ten this was not probable, that Prophet confirms his prediction — <sty c="800000" i="1">I</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">will restore you, says God, I will gather you, even because I have given you</sty>

--- PAGE 116 ---

[BODY] <sty c="800000" i="1">a name</sty>; that is, it is my resolved and fixed purpose to render you celebrated: but here again are laid down the words we have already noticed.

[BODY] He afterwards adds — <sty c="800000" i="1">When I shall restore your captivities</sty>. The plural number is to be noticed; and not rightly nor prudently is what has been done by many interpreters, who have rendered the word in the singular number; for the Prophet mentions captivities designedly, as the Jews had not only been driven into exile, but had also been scattered through various countries, so that they were not one captive people, but many troops of captives. Hence his purpose was to obviate a doubt; for it would not have been enough that one captivity should be restored, except all who had been dispersed were collected into one body by the wonderful power of God. And hence he adds before your eyes, that the Jews might be convinced that they should be eye-witnesses of this miracle, which yet they could hardly conceive, without raising up their thoughts above the world.

--- PAGE 117 ---

[CENTERED_H1] <sty c="0000d4" i="0">FOOTNOTES</sty>

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft1</sty> This clause stands connected with the preceding words; “the
[BODY] stumblingblocks” were the idols, and they were to be taken away “along with the wicked,” according to<sty c="000000" i="1"> Henderson</sty>, and according to the version of <sty c="000000" i="1">Symmachus</sty>, <sty c="0000d4" i="0">sun ajsebe>si</sty>, though <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty>, with less accuracy, renders the words thus, —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">And the stumblingblocks of the wicked.</sty>

[BODY] The whole verse is poetical in its language; the collective singular, and not the plural, is used; and the first verb, [<sty c="008080" i="0">ãµa</sty>], in its most common meaning, is very expressive, and denotes the manner of the ruin that awaited the Jews. They were “gathered” and led into captivity. The two verses may be thus literally rendered, —
[VERSE] <sty c="000000" i="1">2.</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> Gatherings I will gather everything</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">From off the face of the land, saith Jehovah;</sty>
[VERSE] <sty c="000000" i="1">3.</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> I will gather man and best;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">I will gather the bird of heaven and the fish of the sea,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And the stumblingblocks together with the wicked;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And I will cut them off, together with man,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">From the face of the land, saith Jehovah. — Ed.</sty>

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft2</sty> The word is found in two other places, <sty c="800000" i="0"><122305></sty>2 Kings 23:5, and
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><281005></sty>Hosea 10:5. In the latter text the priests of the calf of Bethaven
[BODY] are thus called; in the former, they are said to be those who “burnt incense in the high places.” From this fact, <sty c="000000" i="1">Parkhurst </sty>concludes, that they were called <sty c="000000" i="1">scorched</sty>, as the word means, by their fumigating fires.
[BODY] The “priests” mentioned here were the sacrifices, while the “Camerim” were the incense-burners. There were “altars” (not an altar) reared for Baal in the temple; one, as it seems, for sacrifices, and the other for incense. See <sty c="800000" i="0"><122103></sty>2 Kings 21:3. In <sty c="800000" i="0"><143404></sty>2 Chronicles 34:4, 5, the priests and sacrificers are alone mentioned; but in <sty c="800000" i="0"><122305></sty>2 Kings 23:5, where the same things are recorded, the Camerim and incense are alone named. The Prophet in this passage mentions both.
[CENTERED] Some, as <sty c="000000" i="1">Cocceius </sty>and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>, have been disposed to think that the unfaithful priests of the true God are here meant. But the other

--- PAGE 124 ---

[CENTERED] view is more consistent with the whole passage. If we retain not the original word, we may thus render the line, —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">The name of the incense-burners with the priests;</sty>

[BODY] That is, those who burnt incense and those who offered sacrifices to Baal. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft3</sty> It appears that this idol had two names, Moloc and Milcom, or
[BODY] Molcam. It is called Moloc, or Molec, in <sty c="800000" i="0"><032005></sty>Leviticus 20:5, and in seven other places; but Milcom in <sty c="800000" i="0"><111105></sty>1 Kings 11:5,33; <sty c="800000" i="0"><122314></sty>2 Kings 23:14; as well as here, and also in <sty c="800000" i="0"><244901></sty>Jeremiah 49:1,3, though improperly rendered in our version, “their king.” The Ammonites are the people spoken of.
[BODY] The swearing is here differently expressed: it is <sty c="000000" i="1">to</sty> [(<sty c="008080" i="0">l</sty>)] Jehovah; and by [(<sty c="008080" i="0">b</sty>)] Milcam. To swear <sty c="000000" i="1">to</sty>, is to make a promise to another by an oath, or, in this instance, to swear allegiance to God: but to swear <sty c="000000" i="1">by</sty>, is to appeal to another as witness to an engagement. We have the two forms together in <sty c="800000" i="0"><060919></sty>Joshua 9:19. The Jews made a solemn profession of obedience to God, and yet they acknowledged Melcam as God, by appealing to him as a witness to the truth. It is called the abomination of the Ammonites, <sty c="800000" i="0"><111133></sty>1 Kings 11:33.
[BODY] The image of this god, according to the Rabbins, was hollow, made of brass, and had seven compartments. In the first, they put flour — in the second, turtles — in the third, an ewe — in the fourth, a ram — in the fifth, a calf — in the sixth, an ox — and in the seventh, a child! All these were burnt together by heating the image in the inside! To drown the cries and noises that might be made, they used drums and other instruments. See [<sty c="008080" i="0">˚lm</sty>] in <sty c="000000" i="1">Parkhurst</sty>. How cruel is superstition! and yet how wedded to it is man by nature! Though the Jews had knowledge of the religion of him who is the God of love and mercy; yet they preferred the religion of savages and barbarians. How strongly does this fact prove man’s natural antipathy to God! — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft4</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty> has omitted to notice the last words in the verse, “Nor enquire of
[BODY] him;” which <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>, adopting a modern phraseology, has rendered, “nor apply to him.” The reading ought to be, as many MSS. have it, [<sty c="008080" i="0">whwçrd</sty>]. The verb means to enquire of, to consult, and also to regard or to care for. They did not enquire of God as to his will, or they did not show any regard for him. See Genesis 25: 22; <sty c="800000" i="0"><262001></sty>Ezekiel 20:1;

--- PAGE 125 ---

[BODY] and also <sty c="800000" i="0"><051112></sty>Deuteronomy 11:12; <sty c="800000" i="0"><180304></sty>Job 3:4. To <sty c="000000" i="1">seek</sty> the Lord is to seek his favor and communion with him; to <sty c="000000" i="1">enquire</sty> of the Lord is to seek the knowledge of his will in any difficulty. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft5</sty> The word is [<sty c="008080" i="0">fh</sty>], and is evidently an interjection enjoining silence,
[BODY] Hush! or, Silence!
[INDENT] 7.<sty c="800000" i="1"> Silence at the presence of the Lord Jehovah!</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">For nigh is the day of Jehovah,</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">For prepared hath Jehovah a sacrifice,</sty> <sty c="800000" i="1">Selected hath he his guests!</sty>
[BODY] The passage is remarkably forcible and striking. Jehovah was coming, and everything was prepared, and all were to be silent. And then follows what is no less striking and expressive, —
[VERSE] <sty c="000000" i="1">8.</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> And it shall be in the day of Jehovah’s sacrifice,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">That I will visit the princes and the king’s sons,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And all who wear foreign apparel.</sty>
[VERSE] <sty c="000000" i="1">9.</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> I will also visit, in that day,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Every one who leaps on the threshold,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Who fill the house of their master</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">By plunder and by fraud.</sty>

[BODY] There is in the last line a metonymy; the act is put for what was acquired by it: they filled the house of their master by spoils gained by plunder or violence, and by fraud or cheating. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft6</sty> The first idea of the verb [<sty c="008080" i="0">çdq</sty>], is evidently to set apart, to separate
[BODY] either men or things for a certain purpose. For this meaning <sty c="000000" i="1">Parkhurst</sty> refers to <sty c="800000" i="0"><032024></sty>Leviticus 20:24, compared with version 26, and to
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><051902></sty>Deuteronomy 19:2,7, compared with <sty c="800000" i="0"><062007></sty>Joshua 20:7. This
[BODY] idea seems the most suitable here, “I have set apart (or selected) my guests.” <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> renders it “appointed,” and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>, “consecrated,” as <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty> does. “Segregavit — set apart,” is the version of <sty c="000000" i="1">Drusius</sty>, and <sty c="000000" i="1">Junius</sty> has “preparavit — prepared.” When the verb is followed by “war,” it is rendered “prepare” in our version. See
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><240604></sty>Jeremiah 6:4; <sty c="800000" i="0"><290309></sty>Joel 3:9; <sty c="800000" i="0"><330305></sty>Micah 3:5. The
[BODY] explanation given by <sty c="000000" i="1">Theodosius</sty> is <sty c="0000d4" i="0">ajfwrise</sty> — he separated or selected. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft7</sty> This was a prophecy: though the king Josiah had no children at this
[BODY] time, yet he had some afterwards; and they proved themselves deserving of the judgment here announced, and it was inflicted on them.

--- PAGE 126 ---

[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson’s </sty>objection, that as Josiah had then no children, the prophecy could not apply to them personally, seems wholly inadmissable: it was a <sty c="000000" i="1">prophecy</sty>. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft8</sty> Or, literally, “the garment of a foreigner or stranger,” [<sty c="008080" i="0">yrkn</sty>]. The
[BODY] singular is used poetically for the plural, instead of “the garments of foreigners.” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft9</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius</sty>, following the <sty c="000000" i="1">Septuagint</sty>, and some of the fathers, <sty c="000000" i="1">Cyril</sty>,
[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">Theodoret</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Jerome</sty>, etc., think that the thoughless intruders into the temple are here meant, and such as brought there as sacrifices and gifts the fruits of plunder and fraud. But the passage cannot possibly bear this meaning according to the Hebrew text: nor is such a meaning consistent with the context. The view given here is that of <sty c="000000" i="1">Kimki</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Drusius</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty>, and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft10</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">Junius</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Piscator</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty>, and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> think that it means the
[BODY] second city, a part of Jerusalem, being so called, as they supposed, in
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><161109></sty>Nehemiah 11:9: where our version is considered to be wrong,
[BODY] and the clause ought to be, “and Judah, the son of Jeruiah, was over the second city” — [<sty c="008080" i="0">ry[hAl[ hnçm</sty>]. So it is deemed improperly rendered “college” in <sty c="800000" i="0"><122214></sty>2 Kings 22:14 and <sty c="800000" i="0"><143422></sty>2 Chronicles 34:22; where it ought to be “in the second city.” But the passage in Nehemiah is not decisive on the subject; and our version is countenanced by the former part of the verse, where “Joel” is said to be the “overseer,” and “Judah” is mentioned as being next to him, the second in office: and it is so rendered in the <sty c="000000" i="1">Septuagint. </sty>As to the other text, the word is by itself as here. What <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty>, after <sty c="000000" i="1">Cyril</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Theodoret</sty>, suggests, is the most probable solution.
[CENTERED] The word rendered by <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty> “contritio — breach,” and by <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson, </sty>“destruction,” is [<sty c="008080" i="0">rbç</sty>]. As “crying” and “howling” are said to proceed from the other parts, so something similar must have proceeded from “the hills.” The word means breaking, and it is often applied to the heart — “a broken heart,” <sty c="800000" i="0"><193418></sty>Psalm 34:18; 51:19, etc. It seems to mean here the breaking out into weeping and wailing. The parallelism of the verse would thus be complete —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">And there shall be in that day, saith Jehovah,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">The voice of crying from the fish-gate,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And howling from the second </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">gate,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And great wailing from the hills.</sty>

--- PAGE 127 ---

[BODY] Wailing is the breaking out of anguish and pangs. The word is used in
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><262106></sty>Ezekiel 21:6, for acute pain in the loins, and may be considered
[BODY] as used here metonymically. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft11</sty> This original meaning of the word is much more probable than what
[BODY] lexicographers generally give. The braying or pounding is evidently derived from the noun, and the noun from the form of the mortar. Most agree that the word here means the <sty c="000000" i="1">lower </sty>part of the city — the hollow, from the circumstance of being surrounded by hills. The “hills” were those on which a part of the city was built, such as Zion, Moriah and Ophal. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft12</sty> This opinion has been entertained, because the Jews are so called in
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><281208></sty>Hosea 12:8. That the word means a trader or merchant is
[BODY] evident from <sty c="800000" i="0"><184106></sty>Job 41:6, (in the Hebrew Bibles, 40:30;)
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><232308></sty>Isaiah 23:8; <sty c="800000" i="0"><261704></sty>Ezekiel 17:4. In the last passage it is
[BODY] rendered “traffic” in our version; and it may be so rendered here — “all the people of traffic,” or of trade. The version of <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> is, “all the trafficking people.” The verse may be thus literally rendered, —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Howl ye, the inhabitants of the lower part,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">For reduced to silence have been all the people of trade,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Cut off have been all the laden with silver.</sty>

[BODY] They are called to howl, as though their calamity had already taken place, a mode of speaking often used by the Prophets. That the event was future is clear from the context, especially from the next verse. “Reduced to silence” — [<sty c="008080" i="0">hmdn</sty>], is literally the meaning, not “destroyed;” and appropriate is the term, as people of trade create much bustle and noise. “The laden with silver,” may be rendered, as <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> does, “the bearers of silver:” and silver is here for money. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft13</sty> There is a similar meaning in <sty c="800000" i="0"><244811></sty>Jeremiah 48:11; but the verb is
[BODY] different, [<sty c="008080" i="0">fqç</sty>], which means to be still, to rest, to settle, while the verb here is [<sty c="008080" i="0">apq</sty>], which signifies to be condensed, or to be congealed,
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><021508></sty>Exodus 15:8. But as things congelaed become fixed, the verb
[BODY] seems to have the meaning of fixedness here; as wines on the lees, to which allusion is made, do not become congealed, the comparison seems to be, that as wine kept still on the lees increases in strength and flavor so the Jews, settling on their dregs — their sins — became strengthened and confirmed in their wickedness and atheistic notions.

--- PAGE 128 ---

[BODY] But <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson </sty>take another view of the metaphor, and consider that “the thoughtless tranquillity of the rich is compared to the fixed unbroken surface of fermented liquors.” Our version favors the former idea, as the verb is rendered “settled.” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft14</sty> The Rabbinical punctuation has destroyed the simplicity of this
[BODY] passage by connecting “bitter” with the latter clause. <sty c="000000" i="1">Jerome,</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">Pagninus</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty>, as well as the <sty c="000000" i="1">Septuagint</sty>, connect it with the former clause. The literal rendering of the two lines is as follows —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">The voice of the day of Jehovah shall be grievous;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Roar out there (or then) shall the brave.</sty>

[BODY] “The voice of the day,” etc., means the voice uttered on that day, as <sty c="000000" i="1">Drusius</sty> explains it. [<sty c="008080" i="0">rm</sty>] is no doubt “bitter;” but it is often applied in scripture to express what is grievous, afflictive, or sorrowful. If we render [<sty c="008080" i="0">fç</sty>], “there,” it refers to Jerusalem, verse 12; but it is sometimes used as an adverb of time, “then,” see <sty c="800000" i="0"><191405></sty>Psalm 14:5;
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><160315></sty>Nehemiah 3:15. “The meaning is,” says <sty c="000000" i="1">Drusius</sty>, “that the voice
[BODY] of that day, which they who excel in strength of mind and body shall utter, shall be bitter.” The whole verse is remarkably concise and emphatical, —
[VERSE] <sty c="000000" i="1">14.</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> Nigh is the great day of Jehovah,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Nigh and hastening quickly:</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">The voice of the day of Jehovah shall be grievous;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Roar out then shall the brave.</sty>

[CENTERED] Then the following verse is not to begin, as in our version, which as been followed by <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome </sty>and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>, “That day <sty c="000000" i="1">is </sty>a day of wrath,” but thus —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">A day of wrath </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">shall be</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> that day.</sty>

[CENTERED] This is the order of the original, and as there is no verb, it must be supplied and regulated as to its tense by the context. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft15</sty> The original words are similar in sound and meaning; the first, [<sty c="008080" i="0">hrx</sty>],
[BODY] comes from a verb which means to inclose, to confine, to straiten, and it may be rendered, narrowness, confinement, straitness, distress. The other, [<sty c="008080" i="0">hqwxm</sty>], is oppression, as the verb means to press down, to press close.

--- PAGE 129 ---

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft16</sty> Waste or confusion is, [<sty c="008080" i="0">haç, hawçm</sty>], derived from the same root,
[BODY] may be rendered desolation. The two next words, “darkness” and “thick darkness,” occur in <sty c="800000" i="0"><290202></sty>Joel 2:2. In the same passage we have also “the day of cloudiness and of entire darkness,” literally, bare or naked darkness; for the word is, [<sty c="008080" i="0">lpr[</sty>], derived, as I conceive, from [<sty c="008080" i="0">r[</sty>], bare, and [<sty c="008080" i="0">lpa</sty>], thick darkness. There is a gradation in the words used in each line; the second word is stronger than the first. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft17</sty> Rather “acclamation,” the triumphant voice of conquerors. As an
[BODY] attempt to preserve the distinctive character of each word in this singular passage, I offer the following version —
[VERSE] <sty c="000000" i="1">15.</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> A day of extreme wrath shall be that day,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">A day of distress and oppression,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">A day of waste and of desolation,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">A day of darkness and of thick darkness,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">A day of cloudiness and of entire darkness;</sty>
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">16. A day of the trumpet and of acclamation</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Over the cities that are inclosed,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And over the towers which are lofty.</sty>

[BODY] The word [<sty c="008080" i="0">hrb[</sty>], “extreme wrath,” means such wrath as passes over all bounds — overflowing wrath. We are obliged to use the word darkness three times for lack of suitable terms. The first is the common darkness of the night, the second is a grosser darkness, and the third is complete darkness. The words “gloominess” and “obscurity,” used by <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome </sty>and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>, are not sufficiently strong, and convey not the meaning. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft18</sty> The Hebrew words are literally,
[CENTERED] <sty c="800000" i="1">For against Jehovah have they sinned.</sty> — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft19</sty> “Copiously and in contempt,” says <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius</sty>; “as a thing of no value,”
[BODY] says <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius</sty>; “as worthless as dust,” says <sty c="000000" i="1">Drusius</sty>. The comparison is evidently intended to show that their blood, or their life, would be treated with contempt, and no more regarded than dust. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft20</sty> The word is [<sty c="008080" i="0">fjl</sty>], usually rendered food; here it means what is fed,
[BODY] the carcass, the body. It is rendered “flesh” by the Septuagint. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

--- PAGE 130 ---

[CENTERED_H2] <sty c="006411" i="0">CHAPTER 2</sty>

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft21</sty> Quickness rather than terror is what is evidently meant. See version 14.
[BODY] Most agree in this respect. <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> renders it “speedy,” and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> “sudden.” The word “riddance,” for [<sty c="008080" i="0">hlk</sty>], in our version, is improper. It is rendered “full end” by <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty>, and “consummation” by <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>, and “<sty c="0000d4" i="0">sunte>leian</sty> — end” by the Septuagint. The particle [<sty c="008080" i="0">˚a</sty>] does not mean “altogether,” as rendered by <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>, but it is an asseveration — surely, indeed, certainly, doubtless. The [<sty c="008080" i="0">ja</sty>] before “inhabitants” has evidently here the meaning of <sty c="0000d4" i="0">kata</sty>, with regard to. It is rendered <sty c="0000d4" i="0">epi</sty>, upon, in the Septuagint, and “with” by <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty>. The whole verse is as follows, —
[VERSE] <sty c="000000" i="1">18.</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> Neither their silver nor their gold</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Shall be able to deliver them</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">In the day of the extreme-wrath of Jehovah;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">By the fire of his jealousy</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Shall be consumed the whole land;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">For an end, doubtless sudden, will he make,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">As to all the inhabitants of the land.</sty>

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft22</sty> The verb, found only in five other places — <sty c="800000" i="0"><020507></sty>Exodus 5:7,12;
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><041532></sty>Numbers 15:32,33; and <sty c="800000" i="0"><111710></sty>1 Kings 17:10,12, means to
[BODY] collect, to gather, and not “to search,” as said by <sty c="000000" i="1">Kimchi</sty>, and adopted by <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius</sty>; nor “to bind,” as rendered by <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson. </sty>The import of the passage is considered by all to be an invitation to repentance, though the words are differently rendered. It is difficult to see the meaning when it is said — “Gather yourselves, yea, gather,” etc, except such an assembly is meant as is recommended by <sty c="800000" i="0"><290114></sty>Joel 1:14; the kind of gathering being well understood, it is not mentioned. “Gather yourselves,” that is, to offer prayers, says <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius</sty>. “Be ye assembled — <sty c="0000d4" i="0">suna>cqhte</sty>,” is the rendering of the Septuagint. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft23</sty> [<sty c="008080" i="0">ãsk</sty>] is found as a verb in four other places, <sty c="800000" i="0"><013130></sty>Genesis 31:30;
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><181415></sty>Job 14:15; <sty c="800000" i="0"><191712></sty>Psalm 17:12; and <sty c="800000" i="0"><198403></sty>Psalm 84:3. It means
[BODY] to be or to grow pale, either through love, as in Genesis and Job, or through hunger, as in the first Psalm referred to, or through longing for God’s house, as in the last, or through shame, as some — such as <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Dathius</sty>, and <sty c="000000" i="1">Gesenius</sty>, suppose to be the case here; and they

--- PAGE 131 ---

[BODY] therefore give this rendering — “O nation without shame;” or, “not ashamed.” This idea is favored by the Septuagint — “unteachable — <sty c="0000d4" i="0">ajpai>deuton</sty>.” In no instance is it found in a passive sense as to the feeling through which the paleness is occasioned, and therefore “worthy of love,” or “desired,” cannot be its proper rendering. <sty c="000000" i="1">Buxtorf</sty> give its meaning in Niphal — “desiderio affici — to be touched with or to feel a desire.” Hence the person spoken of is the subject, not the object, of the desire. According, then, to the use of the verb, the rendering here is to be — “Ye nation that feels no desire,” that is, for God and his law, or, “that feels no shame,” that is, for its sins. The paraphrase of the <sty c="000000" i="1">Targum</sty> is — “not willing to be converted to the law,” which corresponds with the idea which has been stated.
[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius</sty> considers that the nation is here described as having “no desire,” that is for that which was good, and that its torpidity and indifference as to religion is what is set forth. And such is the view of <sty c="000000" i="1">Cocceius</sty>; it had no thirst for righteousness, no desire for the kingdom of God — the mark of an unregenerated mind. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft24</sty> It is difficult to make the words bear this sense. Hardly a sentence has
[BODY] been more variously rendered. The most satisfactory solution perhaps is to regard it parenthetic, and to consider “the day” as that allowed for repentance: it was to pass away quickly, like the chaff carried away by the wind —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">As the chaff passing away </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">will be </sty><sty c="000080" i="1">the day:</sty>

[CENTERED] Both <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius </sty>and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> regard this as the meaning. Then the whole verse might be thus translated —
[VERSE] <sty c="000000" i="1">2.</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> Before the bringing forth of the decree,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">(As the chaff passing away will be the day,)</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Before it shall come upon you,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">The burning of Jehovah’s anger;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Before it shall come upon you,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">The day of the anger of Jehovah.</sty>

[CENTERED] Literally it is, “Before it shall not come,” etc., or, “During the time when it shall not come,” etc. [<sty c="008080" i="0">µrfb</sty>] may be rendered “while;” then the version would be —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">While it shall not come upon you,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">The burning of Jehovah’s anger;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">While it shall not come upon you,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">The day of the anger of Jehovah.</sty>

--- PAGE 132 ---

[CENTERED] There are several MSS. which omit the two first lines; but evidently without reason. They are retained in the Septuagint.
[BODY] Possibly the second line may refer to the speedy execution of “the decree,” that its day would pass quickly. Its birth, or its bringing forth was its commencement; and the second line may express its speedy execution: it would be carried into effect with the quickness by which the chaff is carried away by the wind —
[CENTERED] <sty c="000080" i="0">As the chaff passing away will be </sty><sty c="000080" i="1">its </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">day.</sty>

[BODY] The word [<sty c="008080" i="0">rb[</sty>] is, in either case, a participle, and the auxiliary verb is understood, as often is the case in Hebrew, and must partake of the tense of the context. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft25</sty> <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome </sty>renders the adjective “lowly,” and the noun “lowliness;” but
[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> render the first “humble,” as the Septuagint do — <sty c="0000d4" i="0">tapeinoi</sty>, and the second “humility.” They were those who had been made humble by affliction. The design of affliction is to make us humble, submissive to God’s will; and this is the effect of sanctified affliction. It is somewhat singular that the verb means to afflict and to be humble, as though affliction were needful to render us humble. The word [<sty c="008080" i="0">twn[</sty>], occurs in <sty c="800000" i="0"><102236></sty>2 Samuel 22:36, and <sty c="800000" i="0"><191835></sty>Psalm 18:35, and is rendered “gentleness” in our common version, but more correctly in our Prayer-book version “loving correction.” Perhaps the best rendering would be “humbling affliction;” and the idea of humbling affliction making great is very striking. The word used by the Septuagint is <sty c="0000d4" i="0">paideia</sty> — discipline; and the Vulgate is the same. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft26</sty> The idea is not “protected,” as given by <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty>, but “secreted” or
[BODY] concealed as in a hiding-place. “Hid” is the version of <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>, and also of <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius</sty>. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft27</sty> This verse, literally rendered, retains more of its poetic character, —
[VERSE] <sty c="000000" i="1">4.</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> For Gaza, forsaken shall she be,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And Ashkelon shall be a desolation;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Ashdod, at mid-day shall they drive it out,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And Ekron shall be rooted up.</sty>

[CENTERED] In the first and the last line there is a correspondence in the sound of the words.

[BODY] The following presents another instance of the nominative case absolute, —
[VERSE] <sty c="000000" i="1">5.</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> Woe to the dwellers of the line of the sea,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">The nation of the Kerethites!</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">The word of Jehovah is against you:</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Canaan, the land of the Philistines,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">I will even destroy thee, that there shall be no inhabitant.</sty>

[BODY] The <sty c="000000" i="1">line</sty> of the sea, meaning the coast along the shore, is so called, says <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>, “from the custom of using a cord or line in measuring off or dividing a territory.”
[BODY] Some derive “Kerethites” from [<sty c="008080" i="0">trk</sty>], to cut off, to destroy; and so they were cutters off or destroyers. They were celebrated men of war in the time of David, <sty c="800000" i="0"><100818></sty>2 Samuel 8:18. “Philistines” mean emigrants, says <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>; the word being derived from a verb, which signifies, in the Ethiopic language, to rove, to migrate. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft28</sty> The words, [<sty c="008080" i="0">µy[r trk twn</sty>], are rendered by <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty>, “habitaculum
[BODY] caulis pastorum — an habitation (or a dwelling) for the sheepcots of shepherds.” The <sty c="000000" i="1">Targum</sty> takes the two first words in the singular number; the second is evidently so, and the first may be so also: and [<sty c="008080" i="0">trk</sty>] certainly does not mean sheepcots, but digging, from [<sty c="008080" i="0">hrk</sty>], to dig. The reference is either to the pits dug for watering the flock, as <sty c="000000" i="1">Piscator</sty> thinks, o rto the subterraneous huts, or caves, dug for the purpose of shelter, as <sty c="000000" i="1">Drusius</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Bochart </sty>suppose. <sty c="000000" i="1">Junius</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Tremelius</sty> render the words, “sheepcots, the delvings of shepherds;” and <sty c="000000" i="1">Drusius</sty>, “dwellings of the digging out of shepherds,” <sty c="000000" i="1">i.e.</sty>, dwellings dug out by shepherds. The most literal and the easiest construction is, “dwellings, the digging of shepherds.” Then the verse might be thus rendered, —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">And the line of the sea shall be dwellings,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Dug out by shepherds, and folds for sheep.</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">Parkhurst</sty> quotes <sty c="000000" i="1">Harmer</sty>, who says, “the Eastern shepherds make use of <sty c="000000" i="1">caves</sty> very frequently, sleeping in them and driving their flocks into them at night..The mountains bordering on the <sty c="000000" i="1">Syrian</sty> coast are remarkable for the number of caves, and are found particularly in the neighborhood of <sty c="000000" i="1">Ashkelon</sty>.” How fully then was this prophecy fulfilled. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft29</sty> That is, the reproach cast by Moab, and the revilings uttered by
