[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft30</sty> There is a difference as to the meaning of the last line. <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> adopts
[BODY] our common version, —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">And magnified </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">themselves </sty><sty c="000080" i="1">against their border</sty>

[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson’s </sty>rendering is essentially the same —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">And carried themselves haughtily against their border.</sty>

[BODY] The verb [<sty c="008080" i="0">ldn</sty>] is transitive and intransitive in Kal — to make great and to be great; it seems to partake of a similar character in Hiphil, as it is found here, to magnify, and to grow great or proud, and hence to exult or to triumph; and when followed by [<sty c="008080" i="0">l[</sty>], as here, to exult over a person or a country, — see <sty c="800000" i="0"><181905></sty>Job 19:5; <sty c="800000" i="0"><193526></sty>Psalm 35:26; 38:17; <sty c="800000" i="0"><263513></sty>Ezekiel 35:13. In these verses “to exult over” would be the best rendering; as also in 10th verse of this chapter. The idea of enlarging or extending over, as adopted by <sty c="000000" i="1">Jerome</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Dathius</sty>, as well as by <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty>, is not countenanced by any other passage. The best rendering here is —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">And exulted over their border.</sty>

[BODY] This line corresponds with the revilings of Ammon, as the preceding does with the reproach of Moab. That it was the triumphant and exulting language of Ammon is evident, because it was what was <sty c="000000" i="1">heard</sty> — “I have heard,” etc. The particle [<sty c="008080" i="0">rça</sty>], rendered here “quibus — by which,” and “wherewith” by <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty>, is rendered “who” by <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> — “who have reproached on my people;” and this is the most natural construction. Some have rendered it “because.” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft31</sty> This clause is rendered differently by some. The word [<sty c="008080" i="0">qçmm</sty>] occurs
[BODY] only here. It is rendered by the <sty c="000000" i="1">Targum</sty> by a word which means a “deserted place,” and so <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> renders it, “A deserted place for the thorn:” so also do <sty c="000000" i="1">Drusius</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Piscator</sty>, and <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius</sty>. The Septuagint have mistaken the word for “Damascus,” and give a version of the whole clause wholly foreign to the context. <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> thinks that the word has the same meaning with [<sty c="008080" i="0">˚çm</sty>], to draw out, to extend, and gives this version, “A region of overrunning brambles.” This is far-fetched. The word, [<sty c="008080" i="0">lwrj</sty>], rendered “nettle” by <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty>,

--- PAGE 135 ---

[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius</sty>, and others, cannot be so taken, according to <sty c="000000" i="1">Drusius</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Bochart</sty>, for in <sty c="800000" i="0"><183007></sty>Job 30:7, men are said to gather under it. It is found besides only in <sty c="800000" i="0"><202431></sty>Proverbs 24:31. It may be rendered either a thorn or a bramble. The other part of the sentence is literally “a digging place for salt.”
[BODY] Moab was to be like Sodom, and Ammon like Gommorah, not as to the <sty c="000000" i="1">manner</sty> of their ruin, but as to the <sty c="000000" i="1">extent</sty> of it. It was to be an <sty c="000000" i="1">entire</sty> overthrow. Their habitation was not to become a pool of water like Sodom and Gommorah, but a place where the bramble was to grow, and salt might be dug. And it was to be “a desolation,” [<sty c="008080" i="0">µlw[Ad[</sty>], “for ages;” for the word means an indefinite time. So<sty c="000000" i="1"> Drusius</sty> regards it here as meaning a long time. But some consider the “desolation,” as having reference to the people and not to the place. If so, the rendering were wholly obliterated. Moab and Ammon, as a separate people, are altogether extinct. The whole verse is as follows —
[VERSE] <sty c="000000" i="1">9.</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> Therefore, as I live,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Saith Jehovah of hosts, the God of Israel,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Surely Moab like Sodom shall be,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And the children of Ammon like Gomorrah,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">The desert of the thorn and the excavation of salt,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Yea, a desolation for ages;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">The remnant of my people shall plunder them,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And the residue of my nation shall possess them.</sty>

[BODY] The two last lines refer to the children of Ammon, as the two preceding especially to Moab. The country of Moab was on the eastern side of the Dead Sea, and that of Ammon was north-east, of Moab. Both were subdued and led captive by Nebuchadnezzar about four or six years after the captivity of Judah. They were afterwards partially restored, especially the children of Ammon, as Tobiah was their chief in the time of Nehemiah. <sty c="800000" i="0"><160403></sty>Nehemiah 4:3. They were “plundered,” as recorded in 1 Maccabees 5:35,51, by Judas Maccabeus. Of Moab we read nothing at that time: but it appears, that for ages it has been desolate. “Not one,” says <sty c="000000" i="1">Burckhart</sty>, the traveler, “of the ancient cities of Moab exists as tenanted by man,” and he speaks of “their entire desolation.” Another modern traveler, <sty c="000000" i="1">Seetzen</sty>, a Russian, speaking of Ammon, says, “All this country, formerly so populous, is now changed into a vast desert.” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft32</sty> The word, [<sty c="008080" i="0">arwn</sty>], is rendered “to be feared,” by <sty c="000000" i="1">Cocceius</sty> and
[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>, and [<sty c="008080" i="0">µhyl[</sty>], “above them,” that is, “the gods of the earth,” mentioned in the next line; it being considered an instance of a pronoun preceding its noun. But this is forced; and it is not necessary. Moab and Ammon are evidently referred to; and what is said is, that God would be terrible to them, as well as to others, for he would famish or destroy all the gods of the earth. And then in the next verse he mentions other nations. Some extend what is here said to gospeltimes; but there seems no reason for this, inasmuch as God’s judgment is the subject of the Prophet. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft38</sty> The first word, [<sty c="008080" i="0">harwm</sty>], is rendered “rebellions” by <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome </sty>and
[BODY] <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson. </sty>The <sty c="000000" i="1">Vulgate </sty>is nearly the same, “provocatrix — provoking.” The verb is [<sty c="008080" i="0">arm</sty>], once in <sty c="000000" i="1">Hiphil</sty> in <sty c="800000" i="0"><183908></sty>Job 39:8; and to take it to be the same with [<sty c="008080" i="0">hrm</sty>], to rebel, is gratuitous. The context in Job shows its idea to be that of raising up or swelling; and <sty c="000000" i="1">Parkhurst</sty> very properly renders the participle here, swelling, arrogant, insolent; and this notion entirely corresonds with the character given of the city in the next verse; being arrogant, it did “not hear the voice” of God. The verse may be rendered thus —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Woe to the arrogant and polluted,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">The city, which is an oppressor!</sty>

[BODY] Then follows a specification as to her conduct, —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">She has not hearkened to the voice,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">She has not received instruction;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">In Jehovah has she not trusted,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">To her God has she not drawn nigh.</sty>

[BODY] To “obey the voice,” as given in our version and by <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty>, is not quite correct; she was too arrogant even to hear or attend to the voice. “Correction,” as in our version, and by <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty>, is rendered “instruction” by <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>; for [<sty c="008080" i="0">rswm</sty>] has often this meaning. The <sty c="000000" i="1">Septuagint</sty> have <sty c="0000d4" i="0">paidaian</sty> — discipline. But the same phrase occurs in verse 7, where the word necessarily means instruction, by way of warning, communicated by the example of others. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft39</sty> This is the explanation of <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Mede</sty>, and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>. The latter’s
[BODY] version is — “They gnaw no bones in the morning;” <sty c="000000" i="1">i.e.</sty>, all is devoured in the night. <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty>, adopting the conjecture of <sty c="000000" i="1">Houbigant,</sty> supposes the true reading to be [<sty c="008080" i="0">wmdy</sty>], and gives this rendering — “They wait not until the morning,” which seems to have no meaning in this connection. What <sty c="000000" i="1">Cocceius</sty> proposes is more probable — “Who have not gnawed in the morning;” and on this account they were exceedingly voracious in the evening. But the idea of our common version is very appropriate; it implies that they were like wild beasts prowling all night, and carrying as it were their prey to their dens, that they might devour it there in the morning. This is the view taken by <sty c="000000" i="1">Henry.</sty> “They devour the flesh,” says Adam <sty c="000000" i="1">Clarke</sty>, “in the night, and gnaw the bones, and extract the marrow afterwards.” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft40</sty> Her prophets are light, <sty c="000000" i="1">they are </sty>treacherous men. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome.</sty>
[BODY] Her prophets are vainglorious, hypocritical men. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson.</sty>
[BODY] The word rendered “light,” as a river, and not “unstable,” as in our version. It is applied as a participle in <sty c="800000" i="0"><070904></sty>Judges 9:4, to designate persons overflowing in wickedness, dissolute, licentious, dissipated; and as a noun in <sty c="800000" i="0"><242332></sty>Jeremiah 23:32, to set forth the licentious conduct of the false prophets, who like the priests under the Papacy, were given to lasciviousness, and “committed adultery with their neighbors’ wives,” <sty c="800000" i="0"><242923></sty>Jeremiah 29:23. See also <sty c="800000" i="0"><242314></sty>Jeremiah 23:14. As Zephaniah was cotemporary with Jeremiah, his description of the Prophets is thus seen to be the same, “Her Prophets are licentious,” or lascivious.
[BODY] Men of dissimulations or deceits, [<sty c="008080" i="0">twdgb yçna</sty>], signify, that under the pretense of telling the truth, they delivered what was false; or in the words of Jeremiah, they “caused the people to err by their lies,” while they pretended to deliver true messages from God: so that
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><242332></sty>Jeremiah 23:32, contains an explanation of this clause.
[BODY] “Deceiving men” would perhaps be the best rendering. Though they were licentious, yet they deceived men, and made them to believe that they were true Prophets. They were impostors, and notwithstanding their immortal character, they persuaded deluded men that they were true and faithful. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

--- PAGE 139 ---

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft41</sty> The word, [<sty c="008080" i="0">çdq</sty>], as <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty> intimates, does not specifically mean the
[BODY] sanctuary, but holiness, or, as <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> renders it, “what is sacred,” or holy. Both our version and <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> improperly render it “the sanctuary.” The explanation of what is meant may be found in
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0"><261226></sty>Ezekiel 12:26. The word for sanctuary is [<sty c="008080" i="0">çdqm</sty>]. See <sty c="800000" i="0"><262338></sty>Ezekiel 23:38,39.
[BODY] The words, [<sty c="008080" i="0">hrwt wsmj</sty>], have been taken to mean, — either, “They violated the law,” as the words are rendered in <sty c="800000" i="0"><261226></sty>Ezekiel 12:26, that is, transgressed it by acting contrary to it; or, “They perverted the law,” forcing it, as it were, out of its plain meaning by subtle glosses. The <sty c="000000" i="1">Septuagint</sty> render the verb <sty c="0000d4" i="0">hqethsan</sty> — set aside or abolished, in Ezekiel, and here <sty c="0000d4" i="0">ajsebousi</sty> — act impiously. “Trangressed,” says <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius</sty>; “Do violecne to,” say <sty c="000000" i="1">Piscator</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Drusius</sty>, that is, by wresting its words. It occurs much oftener as a noun than as a verb, and it commonly means a wrong or injustice done in an outrageous and violent manner. According to this general idea, we may render the phrase here, “they have outraged the law,” either by their conduct, or by their comments. It was in either case a wrong done to the law, that was enormous, passing all reason and decency. So that to transgress or to violate, or to do violence to, or to pervert the law, does not convey the full meaning. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft42</sty> The verbs here are in the future tense, but evidently express, as <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty>
[BODY] observes, a continued act. The same is exactly the case in <sty c="000000" i="1">Welsh</sty>; the verbs are in the future tense, but are understood as expressing a present act or a continued act, or what is continually or habitually done. In English the present must be adopted —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">The righteous Jehovah </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">is </sty><sty c="000080" i="1">in the midst of her,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">He doeth no injustice:</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Every morning his judgment</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">He bringeth to light — it fails not;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Yet the unjust knoweth no shame.</sty>

[BODY] “Injustice” in the second, and “unjust” in the fifth line, come from the same root. “Judgment” here is what God judges and determines to be right and just; and it is set forth here as the sun rising every day from morning to morning, and as never failing to appear. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft43</sty> This verse, literally rendered, is as follows, —

--- PAGE 140 ---

[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">I have cut off nations;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Desolate are become their towers;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">I have made solitary their streets, without a passenger;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Deserted are become their cities,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Without a man, without an inhabitant.</sty>

[CENTERED] It is not the <sty c="000000" i="1">destruction</sty>. The nations being cut off, then the towers became desolate, the streets empty, and the cities forsaken. The last line but one is literally — “Hunted have been their cities,” so that no man was left behind. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft44</sty> The last clause has been variously rendered. There is no assistance
[BODY] from the Septuagin, as the whole text is very different. <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius</sty>, after <sty c="000000" i="1">Drusius</sty>, connects it, not with the preceding, but with the following line, in this sense, that how much soever God had punished the city, yet its inhabitants were the more best to corrupt their ways. But the words can hardly admit of this meaning. <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> supposes [<sty c="008080" i="0">k</sty>] to be understood before [<sty c="008080" i="0">lk</sty>], and gives this rendering of the two lines —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">That her habitation might not be cut off,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">According </sty><sty c="000080" i="1">to all that I had appointed concerning her.</sty>

[INDENT] <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome </sty>differs as to the last line —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">After all </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">the punishment with</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> which I had visited her.</sty>

[BODY] None of these are satisfactory. <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius</sty>, taking the sense of the <sty c="000000" i="1">Targum, </sty>means to have given the best meaning. He says that [<sty c="008080" i="0">dqp</sty>], followed by [<sty c="008080" i="0">l[</sty>], means sometimes to appoint or constitute, and refers to <sty c="800000" i="0"><143623></sty>2 Chronicles 36:23, “All the <sty c="000000" i="1">good </sty>which I have appointed to her,” or promised; but he unnecessarily supposes “shall come” to be understood; for the word, “all which,” may be considered to be in apposition with “habitation.” I give the following version of this whole verse —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">I said, “Surely thou wilt fear me,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Thou wilt receive instruction;”</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Then cut off should not be her habitation —</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">All that I have committed to her:</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Yet they rose up early, they corrupted all their doings.</sty>

[CENTERED] To rise up early is a Hebrew phrase, which means a resolved and diligent attention to a thing. The import of the line is, that they with full-bent purpose and activity corrupted all their doings. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>

--- PAGE 141 ---

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft45</sty> This verse is considered by <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty> to be addressed
[BODY] to the godly, to encourage them at the approaching calamities, while <sty c="000000" i="1">Piscator</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius</sty>, and <sty c="000000" i="1">Dathius</sty>, agree with <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty> that it is an awful warning to the wicked Jews, spoken of in the preceding verse. Differing somewhat from <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty>, they regard the “nations” and “kingdoms” to be the Babylonians, who were composed of various nations and kingdoms, and “upon them” to be the Jews, and “the whole land” to be that of Judea. This view, no doubt, is the most consistent with the context. The objection made by <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>, that the words <sty c="000000" i="1">expect</sty>, or <sty c="000000" i="1">wait for me</sty>, are ever used in a good sense, seems to have no force, for these words by themselves can mean neither what is good nor what is bad, the whole depends on the context. The verb [<sty c="008080" i="0">hkj</sty>] simply means to tarry, to wait — <sty c="0000d4" i="0">menein</sty>. The word “therefore” seems to connect this with the preceding verse, and there is nothing in the foregoing part of the chapter that alludes to the godly. Besides, the words which follow “wait for me” explain them, as will be seen by the following literal rendering of the whole verse —
[VERSE] <sty c="000000" i="1">8.</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> Therefore wait for me, saith Jehovah,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">For the day of my rising to the prey!</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">For my purpose is to gather nations,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">To assemble kingdoms,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">In order to pour on them my indignation,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">All the heat of my anger;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">For by the fire of my jealousy</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Shall be consumed the whole land.</sty>

[BODY] The “fire of God’s jealousy” sufficiently proves that what is meant is the land of Judea. (See chapter 1:18.) — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft46</sty> [<sty c="008080" i="0">ˆa yk</sty>], “For then,” <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>; “Surely then,” <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty>; “Postea
[BODY] vero — but afterwards,” <sty c="000000" i="1">Dathius</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius</sty>. And <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> says, that [<sty c="008080" i="0">ˆa</sty>] is used here largely, for “afterwards.” It refers to the time after the execution of the judgments previously mentioned.
[BODY] “The pure lip” is evidently not the language which God would adopt in addressing the nations, but the language they would adopt in addressing him. What is meant is a pure heart; what gives utterance to the heart is mentioned for the heart itself; as the “shoulder” is afterwards used for the service that is rendered to God.

--- PAGE 142 ---

[BODY] The verb [<sty c="008080" i="0">˚ph</sty>], to turn, means to change the form, condition, or course of a thing, conveying perhaps here the idea, that the pure lip is substituted for that which is impure: “I will give them as a change, instead of what they have, a pure lip.” <sty c="0000d4" i="0">Metastreyw</sty> — “ I will change,” Sept. and Sym.; <sty c="0000d4" i="0">streyw</sty> — “ I will turn,” Aq. and Theod. It is rendered “reddam” and “restituam” by <sty c="000000" i="1">Drusius </sty>and <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius</sty>.
[CENTERED] <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty>, following the conjecture of <sty c="000000" i="1">Houbigant</sty>, reads [<sty c="008080" i="0">˚pça</sty>], “I will pour out,” contrary to all the ancient versions, and without the countenance of a single MS.
[BODY] Though the word, [<sty c="008080" i="0">µym[</sty>], peoples, most frequently means the nations, yet there are instances in which it means the people of Israel, inasmuch as they were composed of various tribes. See <sty c="800000" i="0"><112228></sty>1 Kings 22:28; <sty c="800000" i="0"><290206></sty>Joel 2:6. And if we render the verb, “restore,” with <sty c="000000" i="1">Drusius</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius</sty>, then we must adopt this meaning. Eleven MSS. have “and,” [<sty c="008080" i="0">w</sty>], before the verb to “serve:” and as there is no preposition before “shoulder,” we may render the verse —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">But I will then restore to the people a pure lip,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">That they may, all of them, call on the name of Jehovah, —</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And one shoulder, that they may serve him. — </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">Ed.</sty>

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft47</sty> The expression “with one shoulder” is rendered by the Septuagint,
[BODY] “under one yoke” — <sty c="0000d4" i="0">uJpo< zugo<n e[na</sty>. The idea is that of oxen drawing together. To serve God under one yoke, is to do the same service unitedly. “A metaphor,” says <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty>, “from the joint efforts of yoked beasts.” — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft48</sty> It is more consonant with the style of the Prophets to render the
[BODY] clauses apart, as <sty c="000000" i="1">Calvin</sty> does, than as it is done in our version, and by <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>. The auxiliary verb, as is often the case, is to be understood in the first clause, —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">From beyond the rivers of Cush </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">shall be </sty><sty c="000080" i="1">my suppliants;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">The daughter of my dispersed shall bring my offering.</sty>

[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft49</sty> This may be rendered, “Those who exult in thy exaltation:” the Targum
[BODY] has it, “in thy glory.” This “glory” or “exaltation,” as explained in the next verse, was Mount Sion. There was a preeminence, but it was made an object of unholy boasting. The paraphrase of <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>, “thy proud exulters,” completely leaves out the character of their exultation. The whole verse may be thus rendered, —

--- PAGE 143 ---

[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">In that day thou shalt not be ashamed of thy doings,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">By which thou hast transgressed against me;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">For then will I remove from the midst of thee</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Those who exult in thy exaltation;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">And thou shalt no more be elevated</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">On account of the mount of my holiness.</sty>

[BODY] The word [<sty c="008080" i="0">twag</sty>] means exaltation or glory in a good as well as in a bad sense. See <sty c="800000" i="0"><199301></sty>Psalm 93:1; <sty c="800000" i="0"><231205></sty>Isaiah 12:5. What they exulted in was in itself good, but they exulted only in an outward privilege, without connecting it with God, as many have done in all ages. This is the essence of Pharisaism. <sty c="000000" i="1">Vatables</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Drusius</sty> regard the word as having this sense here. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft50</sty> The first word, [<sty c="008080" i="0">yk[</sty>], means one made humble by distress or
[BODY] affliction, the humbled, rather than the humble. The second word, [<sty c="008080" i="0">ld</sty>], is one exhausted, or reduced in number, or reduced to poverty. <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> renders it “lowly,” but improperly. <sty c="000000" i="1">Jerome</sty> has “pauperem et egenum — poor and needy;” the Septuagint, “<sty c="0000d4" i="0">praun kai tapeinon</sty> — meek and humble;” <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius</sty>, “afflictum et attenuatum — afflicted and diminished.” Perhaps the best rendering would be, “a people humbled and reduced.” The idea of being “afflicted” or distressed, is excluded by what is expressed at the end of the next verse, and also that of being “poor” in a worldly respect. The reference seems to be to a humbled state of mind, occasioned by calamities, and to a reduced number — a remnant.
[BODY] “I will leave” for [<sty c="008080" i="0">ytraçh</sty>], as in our version, is not its full meaning. It means to reserve as a remnant. “I will cause to remain,” or, “I will reserve,” would be the proper rendering. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft51</sty> To give the words their specific meaning, they may be thus rendered,
[BODY] —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Cry aloud thou daughter of Zion,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Shout ye Israel;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Rejoice and exult with all thine heart,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Thou daughter of Jerusalem.</sty>

[BODY] The first two lines encourage the fullest expression of feelings, loud crying, and shouting like a trumpet; and then is set forth the character of these feelings; they were to be those of job and exultation. Our version, <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome </sty>and <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson</sty>, render the second line correctly, but

--- PAGE 144 ---

[BODY] not the first; and “Be glad and rejoice” are too feeble to express what the third line contains: for the exhortation is to “rejoice” and to “exult.” It was to be the loud cry of joy, and the shouting of exultation or triumph. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">ft52</sty> Turned aside hath Jehovah thy judgments. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft53</sty> The words are, [<sty c="008080" i="0">˚bya hnp</sty>], “he hath turned away thine enemy.”
[BODY] Many copies have [<sty c="008080" i="0">˚ybya</sty>], “thine enemies;” but it may be regarded as the poetical singular. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft54</sty> This is a very remarkable passage. Perhaps the more literal version
[BODY] would be the following, —
[VERSE] <sty c="000000" i="1">16.</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> In that day he will say to Jerusalem, “Fear thou not;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Sion! relaxed let not thy hands be:</sty>
[VERSE] <sty c="000000" i="1">17.</sty><sty c="000080" i="1"> He will rejoice over thee with joy;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">He will renew thee in his love,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">He will exult over thee with acclamation.”</sty>

[BODY] The verb [<sty c="008080" i="0">rmay</sty>] is rendered as above by the Septuagint, <sty c="0000d4" i="0">erei</sty>, meaning the Lord. The last line but one is according to the Septuagint and the Syriac; and this sense has been adopted by <sty c="000000" i="1">Houbigant</sty>, <sty c="000000" i="1">Dathius</sty>, and <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty>. There is the difference only of one letter, [<sty c="008080" i="0">d</sty>] for [<sty c="008080" i="0">r</sty>], which are very like. The law of parallelism is in favor of this meaning. The verse contains four lines: there is an evident correspondence of meaning in the second and the last line; and so there is between the first and the third according to the preceding version, but not otherwise. The word rendered “acclamation” is a noun from the verb [<sty c="008080" i="0">hkr</sty>], to cry aloud, used at the beginning of verse 14. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft55</sty> This verse presents considerable difficulties, and has been variously
[BODY] rendered. The Septuagint and the Targum differ as much from one another, as they do from the Hebrew. None regard the former as at all suitable; but some, as <sty c="000000" i="1">Grotius</sty> and <sty c="000000" i="1">Dathius</sty>, take the meaning of the latter, though to reconcile it with the Hebrew is difficult. <sty c="000000" i="1">Marckius</sty> seems to have given the most probable meaning —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Remotos a festivitate collegi,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">Ex to sunt, onus super eam opprobrium.</sty>
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">Those driven away from festivity have I gathered,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">From thee they are — a burden on her </sty><sty c="000080" i="0">is </sty><sty c="000080" i="1">reproach.</sty>

--- PAGE 145 ---

[BODY] The word [<sty c="008080" i="0">ygwn</sty>], he derives from [<sty c="008080" i="0">hgh</sty>]. In this case it is literally, “my driven away,” or, “my removed” ones. [<sty c="008080" i="0">d[wm</sty>] is assembling or meeting, as well as a fixed time or season; and the assembling was that on festal days: it may therefore be rendered, “festivals.” “From thee” is “Sion” in verse 16. Instead of “on her,” more than ten copies, as well as the Targum, have “on thee,” [<sty c="008080" i="0">˚yl[</sty>]; but an abrupt change of person is of frequent occurrence in the Prophets.
[BODY] Following the sense of the Targum, we may, perhaps, give the following version —
[VERSE] <sty c="000080" i="1">The grieved for the festivals have I gathered from thee;</sty> <sty c="000080" i="1">They were a burden on thee, a reproach.</sty>

[BODY] The paraphrase of the Targum, as given by <sty c="000000" i="1">Dathius</sty>, is the following —
[CENTERED] <sty c="000080" i="0">Those who among thee have impeded the seasons of thy festivity,</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">I will expel from thee; wo to them who have carried arms against</sty> <sty c="000080" i="0">thee, and loaded thee with reproaches.</sty>

[BODY] The “grieved for the festivals” were those who disliked them, who grudged the offerings that were to be made. The words are in the past tense, but future as to what is said; for the Prophets declare things as exhibited to them in a vision. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
[FOOTNOTE] <sty c="800000" i="0">Ft56</sty> The first clause in this verse is amended by <sty c="000000" i="1">Newcome</sty> and some other
[BODY] in conformity with the Septuagint: but this is a very unsafe process. <sty c="000000" i="1">Henderson’s </sty>version is —
[BODY] Behold, I will deal with all thine oppressors at that time. “Deal,” [<sty c="008080" i="0">hç[</sty>]; “interficiam — I will slay,” <sty c="000000" i="1">Vulg.</sty>; “conficiam — I will make an end,” <sty c="000000" i="1">Drusius</sty>; but to “deal with,” or “act against,” is the literal rendering. More is implied than what is expressed, which is often the case with words used in every language. — <sty c="000000" i="1">Ed.</sty>
